\section{Molecular representations, models and label noise}\label{app:add:background}

\addfile{Tolerance band for the delivered noise}
\label{app:add:dose_tolerance}
% [CUTS 2026-10-10, item 10] Moved unchanged from thesis.tex, Chapter 2, The NoiseInject package, Regression noise.
% TODO: manual review
The delivered noise is checked against a band of three
standard errors,
\begin{equation}
\left|\frac{\hat{\tau}}{\tau} - 1\right| \le \max\!\left(3\sqrt{\frac{\kappa - 1}{4\,n_{\mathrm{eff}}}},\; 0.005\right),
\qquad
n_{\mathrm{eff}} = \frac{\left(\sum_i s_i^2\right)^2}{\sum_i s_i^4},
\label{eq:dose_tolerance}
\end{equation}
where $\hat{\tau}$ is the delivered root-mean-square noise and $\kappa$ is the sample kurtosis of the
unscaled draws $z$, bounded between 3 and 60. The effective count $n_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is the number of
independent contributions the delivered noise averages over, and falls below $n$ when the noise is
concentrated on a few molecules. Under grouped-shifted it is
$n_{\mathrm{eff}} = \left(\rho^2/g_{\mathrm{eff}} + (1-\rho)^2/n\right)^{-1}$, with
$g_{\mathrm{eff}} = \left(\sum_g n_g\right)^2 / \sum_g n_g^2$ for family sizes $n_g$. At
$\nu \le 4$ the Student-$t$ distribution has no fourth moment, so the standard error is instead
$1.3\,n_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\,2/\nu - 1}$, with the constant calibrated on the measured spread. A draw
outside the band raises a warning, which a correct injector does on about $1\%$ of draws; the delivered amount is recorded either way.

\addfile{Hyperparameters for every model configuration, and the descriptors that make up PDV}
\label{app:noise:sec:hyperparameters_default}\label{app:noise:sec:hyperparameters_tuned}\label{app:noise:sec:pdv_descriptors}
Table~A lists the default settings, which both the QM9 and the assay pipelines read from one file. Table~B lists the tuned settings that replace a default, per model and per dataset, on QM9, LogD, Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$. Table~C lists the 200 descriptor names that make up PDV, in the order they are computed.

\addfile{Memory-mapped data files of the QM9 pipeline}
\label{app:add:mmap}
% [CUTS 2026-10-10, item 11] Moved unchanged from thesis.tex Chapter 3 (QM9 data pipeline); the thesis keeps one sentence.
% TODO: manual review
% TODO: check the figure: its table gives ECFP4 as 32 bytes and Sort & Slice as 16 bytes; the old text said 256 and 2,048 bytes.
Rather than parse a CSV or Parquet file into memory for every replicate, our pipeline writes the preprocessed data to memory-mapped binary files (\texttt{.mmap}), which stay on disk and are read record by record (Figure~\ref{fig:fig_mmap}). Python writes one file per split, and the Rust program writes a copy of it in which the processed labels replace the raw ones; Python reads that copy for training. Each record holds the isomeric and canonical SMILES, the raw and processed target values, and the requested representations as fixed-width blocks. Python and Rust read the same pages on disk without deserialization.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figure_mmap_structure.png}
    \caption[In-memory and memory-mapped data loading.]{In-memory (top left) and memory-mapped (bottom left) data loading. Memory-mapped loading keeps each molecule as one record of a binary file on disk and reads records into memory on demand. The table on the right lists the fields of one record.}
    \label{fig:fig_mmap}
\end{figure}

\section{The flaws of the similarity assumption}\label{app:add:flaws}
% [notes moved to thesis_notes_moved.md]

One row of Table~\ref{tab:flaws:distance_per_rep} is one representation on one dataset. Representations are listed in each dataset's pool order.

\begin{longtable}{llrrrrr}
\caption{All-pairs correlation, five-nearest-neighbor prediction and activity cliffs for every representation in each regression dataset's pool. All pairs: Spearman correlation between pair distance and absolute label difference, on a fixed sample of 2,000 molecules (seed 0) where a dataset has more. Five neighbors: Spearman correlation and RMSE (in the label's units) of the mean label of the five nearest molecules from other scaffold groups. Cliff molecules: molecules in at least one of this representation's closest pairs whose labels differ by more than the dataset's gap (Table~\ref{tab:flaws:distance_summary}). The number of closest pairs is the number ECFP4 has at Tanimoto $\geq 0.55$, a few more where distances tie at the cut-off. Shared: percentage of ECFP4's similar pairs that are also among this representation's closest pairs. HLM clearance's pool has no ECFP4.}\label{tab:flaws:distance_per_rep}\\

% [TRIM2 2026-09-30] The paragraphs below were moved verbatim from Chapter 5 (c5_hybrid-P5). Only the paragraph headings are new,
% [TRIM2 2026-09-30] and in the first paragraph 'Additional file~\ref{app:add:design_experiments}' became 'Table~\ref{tab:add:design_experiments}', since it now sits in that file.
% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{Values of the search settings.}
% TODO: REVIEW MODIFICATION
Before the full runs, we ran a series of preliminary design experiments to determine the values each setting of the search may take. Each used a few datasets and 1 to 5 folds, and each dataset was scored in its own metric, with scores never averaged across datasets. When making a selection, we considered whether a value gave the best score for some dataset, downstream model and budget, as well as the compute time in hours. A value that won somewhere but cost many hours stayed in the full search and was left out of the fast tier. Each setting's values were then either kept, because each won for some dataset, model and budget, or fixed at one value. A setting was fixed when the spread between its values was smaller than the spread of one value across folds. As an example of a kept value, nine downstream models were fitted on each of 10 single representations over 5 folds. The datasets were Buchwald--Hartwig yield, Caco-2 efflux, Koenig expression and LogD. The DNN scored best on 3 of the 4 datasets and third on Caco-2 (0.2936 against 0.2851 RMSE, log$_{10}$ efflux ratio), so it was added. As an example of a fixed rule, we tested five ways of rescaling each representation's score before the budget is split. On hERG $K_i$, with linear allocation at 300 features, the five rescalings gave RMSE values from 0.5870 to 0.5924 (p$K_i$). A single rescaling, however, ranged from 0.5475 to 0.6294 RMSE across the 5 folds, so rescaling is one fixed rule per metric. Every design experiment is listed in Table~\ref{tab:add:design_experiments}.

% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{Importance methods.}
% CODE INPUT
Two comparisons were run, the first of how the methods rank features and the second of the hybrids those rankings build. The ranking comparison scored 8 importance methods on 5 datasets, namely LogD, hERG $K_i$, Buchwald--Hartwig yield, nanobody thermostability and antibody FcRn retention. For each pair of methods, it measured the Spearman correlation between their scores over every feature of every representation. The three retained methods rank features differently. This comparison is preliminary, because each method was fitted once on the whole dataset rather than inside folds.

% CODE INPUT
The hybrid comparison used Caco-2, with 2,158 molecules, 10 representations, 3 scaffold folds and a budget of 100 features. Hybrids were built from each method's ranking and scored with random forest and LightGBM downstream models. Three control rankings were added, namely feature variance, Laplacian score and a random order. The measure was the RMSE of the best single representation minus the RMSE of the hybrid, in log$_{10}$ efflux ratio units, so that a positive value means the hybrid has the lower error. The preliminary results were $+0.0144$ for DeepLIFT, $+0.0100$ for LightGBM gain, $+0.0067$ for TreeSHAP and $-0.0118$ for the random ranking. The gap between DeepLIFT and LightGBM gain, 0.0044 RMSE, is smaller than the fold-to-fold spread. The methods also differ at the top of their rankings. Of each representation's 100 top-ranked features on Caco-2, the retained pairs of methods share a median of 28 to 54 features.

% CODE INPUT
Three rules determined which methods were retained. First, a method must score every feature of every representation for regression, binary and multiclass labels, which all three retained methods do. Second, of two methods whose rankings agree above a Spearman correlation of 0.95, we keep the faster one. Under this rule, DeepLIFT replaced integrated gradients, which agreed with it above 0.95 on 4 of 6 datasets, and gradient SHAP, which agreed above 0.95 on 3 of 6. Third, we keep methods that rank features differently, because a different ranking builds a different hybrid. TreeSHAP came last of the three on Caco-2 and was kept under this third rule. Only two filters act on features before allocation, the cut at a score of 0 and the pre-screen on representations wider than 2,000 features. On a 2,048-bit fingerprint, the pre-screen drops the 48 lowest-variance bits. On hERG $K_i$ all 48 are among the 105 constant ECFP4 bits, while on QM9, which has no constant ECFP4 bits, they are near-constant ones. Near-constant features otherwise remain candidates and are ranked by importance.

% CODE INPUT
% [PLACEHOLDER removed from printed text 2026-10-10] \textbf{[RESULT NEEDED: the same win counts, per dataset and importance method, from the full runs]}
No single importance method suits every dataset and downstream model. A preliminary run used 4 datasets (nanobody thermostability, hERG $K_i$, humanness and LogD), 5 folds, 10 representations, 5 downstream models and budgets of 25, 50, 100, 150 and 300 features. For each of the 25 pairs of downstream model and budget per dataset, we counted which importance method gave the best configuration, and Table~\ref{tab:importance_wins} gives the counts. LightGBM gain won the most pairs on two datasets and tied with TreeSHAP on humanness. DeepLIFT won the most on one dataset, LogD, with 17 of its 25 pairs, and outside LogD it gave the best configuration for 1 of 75 pairs. On LogD, the DNN took DeepLIFT at 3 of 5 budgets. On LogD, XGBoost and LightGBM each took DeepLIFT at 5 of 5 budgets, and random forest took LightGBM gain at 5 of 5.

% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{Allocation methods.}
% CODE INPUT
We first tried greedy forward selection, which picks features for how well they work together, something fixed shares cannot do \citep{Kohavi1997, Guyon2003}. In each round, it added the next block of features from whichever representation most improved the internal validation score. It stopped when the budget was spent or when the score had not improved for a set number of rounds. With blocks of $s$ features and $K$ representations, it needs up to $(B/s) \times K$ fits per configuration and fold. With blocks of 10 features, this amounts to 500 fits at 500 features and 10 representations, against 10 for the score-based methods. In preliminary runs, it reached a Spearman correlation of 0.3600 on Jain antibody FcRn retention, against 0.4526 for linear allocation, and placed 16th of 17 methods. It was removed from the search.

% CODE INPUT
The five methods were chosen in a preliminary comparison run before the main experiments. It compared 8 candidate allocation methods, each at 4 representation counts, on 9 regression datasets. It used 3 downstream models (LightGBM, random forest and CatBoost) and budgets of 500 and 1,000 features, giving 54 combinations of dataset, downstream model and budget. A fixed-share method was the single best method in 38 of the 54 combinations and the global pool in 16. All five were kept because no single method was best in most combinations. This comparison predates the current score rescaling, and it sized the weights with an inner 3-fold group split instead of the 80/20 internal split used now.

% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{Matched internal evaluator.}
% [TRIM 2026-09-30] shortened
% CODE INPUT
% [PLACEHOLDER removed from printed text 2026-10-10] \textbf{[RESULT NEEDED: on these 4 datasets, how many of the 20 pairs of dataset and downstream model, and of the 100 combinations with budget, the matched evaluator gave the best score, and the number expected with no effect of matching]}
% [PLACEHOLDER removed from printed text 2026-10-10] \textbf{[RESULT NEEDED: per dataset and downstream model, how often the winning configuration used the matched evaluator, and its gap to the best other evaluator in the dataset's metric]}
Matching the evaluator to the downstream model is the obvious default. However, the evaluator only has to rank representations, and another model may rank them better for the downstream model. Because the matched evaluator is one of the four searched values, the search already covers this default, and a mismatched evaluator winning is informative rather than a negative result. We first counted wins in the search itself, in a preliminary run on 4 datasets (nanobody thermostability, hERG $K_i$, humanness and LogD). These runs used 10 representations, budgets of 25 to 300 features and 5 folds, and their scores are the search's own fold scores rather than held-out scores. On nanobody thermostability with a DNN downstream model, the matched evaluator reached a Spearman correlation of 0.6696 against 0.6506 for the LightGBM evaluator, a gap of 0.0190. Its 5 fold scores ranged from 0.5095 to 0.7920. On LogD with an SVM downstream model, the matched evaluator scored 0.4322 RMSE (log units) against 0.4217 for the LightGBM evaluator, where lower is better. This is a gap of 0.0105, the matched evaluator lost at all 5 budgets, and its 5 fold scores ranged from 0.4118 to 0.4634. We then scored the evaluators on held-out folds in a second preliminary run, with 3 outer folds, a budget of 250 features, 5 representations and LightGBM gain only. The downstream models were random forest and SVM. On nanobody thermostability with a random forest downstream model, the matched evaluator reached a Spearman correlation of 0.6098, the LightGBM evaluator 0.6220 and the SVM evaluator 0.6172. The matched evaluator's fold scores ranged from 0.5659 to 0.6734. With an SVM downstream model, the matched evaluator reached 0.6189, the Gaussian process evaluator 0.6265 and the LightGBM evaluator 0.6317, with fold scores for the matched evaluator from 0.5530 to 0.6703. On hERG $K_i$ with a random forest downstream model, the matched evaluator scored 0.6046 RMSE (p$K_i$), the SVM evaluator 0.6059 and the LightGBM evaluator 0.6075. Its fold scores ranged from 0.5957 to 0.6178, and it beat the SVM evaluator on 2 of 3 folds. With an SVM downstream model, the matched evaluator scored 0.5723 RMSE (p$K_i$), the LightGBM evaluator 0.5724 and the Gaussian process evaluator 0.5765, with fold scores for the matched evaluator from 0.5605 to 0.5862. Every gap above, both held out and within the search, is smaller than the range of the matched evaluator's own fold scores.

% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{Fast tier.}
% CODE INPUT
% [PLACEHOLDER removed from printed text 2026-10-10] \textbf{[RESULT NEEDED: range over the 4 datasets]}
% [PLACEHOLDER removed from printed text 2026-10-10] \textbf{[RESULT NEEDED: the share of run time the fast tier and adaptive pruning saved on the full runs]}
The fast lists were chosen by one rule in a preliminary comparison. A value stays in the fast lists if it is inexpensive and improves the best score, whereas an expensive value that wins often stays in the full search only. Importance methods and downstream models were measured on four datasets, with 5 folds each, against the unpruned full search. The four were nanobody thermostability, humanness, hERG $K_i$ and LogD. DeepLIFT took about half of the fast run's hours on all four datasets, and dropping it changed only LogD's best score, from 0.4905 to 0.4930 RMSE (log units). Adding the DNN as a downstream model moved LogD from 0.4930 to 0.4273 RMSE, against a full-search best of 0.4170. Adding the SVM moved hERG $K_i$ from 0.5776 to 0.5599 RMSE (p$K_i$), while adding XGBoost changed no dataset's best score. All five downstream models are now searched at both tiers. The global pool gave the best configuration for some combinations of dataset, downstream model and budget on those four datasets. It is expensive, however, so it is in the full search only. The three fast allocation methods are linear, squared and rank-decay allocation.

\addfile{Worked example of fold counts}
\label{app:add:fold_counts}
When a training fold holds fewer than five groups, both splits use as many folds as there are groups. For example, Koenig expression has five groups, so each outer training fold holds four. The search inside that fold uses 4 folds of three groups each, and the internal split holds out one of the three.

\addfile{Fields of a blueprint and their types}
\label{app:add:blueprint_listing}
\begin{lstlisting}[float=htbp, breaklines=true, basicstyle=\scriptsize\ttfamily, columns=fullflexible, keepspaces=true, frame=single, caption={Fields of a blueprint and their types. Comment lines group the fields, and each field is given with its type and, where needed, a comment on its meaning.}, label=lst:blueprint, captionpos=b]
Blueprint
  # What the search chose. A rebuild reads all of these.
  reps                  : list[str]           # every representation searched, not only those used
  rep_count             : int or None         # how many of them may contribute; None = all
  budget                : int                 # most features the hybrid may have
  allocation_method     : str
  importance_method     : str
  internal_evaluator    : str
  objective             : str                 # 'predictive' or 'similarity'

  # How the search scored configurations.
  metric                : str
  lower_is_better       : bool
  task_type             : str                 # 'regression' or 'classification'
  n_folds               : int
  seed                  : int

  # Augmentation settings. A rebuild reads all of these.
  augmentation_strategy : str
  aug_per_source_budget : int, or {source: int}, or None
  augmentation_margin   : float
  augmentation_fraction : float or None
  candidate_ranking     : str
  augmentation_threshold: float or None
  augmentation_budget   : int
  aug_max_candidates    : int

  # Stored for inspection.
  rep_scores            : {representation: float}
                          # each representation's best score over every molecule the search saw;
                          # the rebuild uses it to choose which representations to keep only when
                          # rep_count is set and no per-fold scores are given
  allocation_per_fold   : {fold: {representation: int}}      # features each representation got
  kept_augmentations    : {fold: {source: list[int]}}         # extra features kept, by index
\end{lstlisting}

% [CUTS 2026-10-10, item 14] moved unchanged from Chapter 5, "Augmentation"; the chapter keeps its first paragraph and the result of the earlier search.
\addfile{How augmentation ranks and adds donor features}
\label{app:add:augmentation}
% TODO: manual review
Donors are set per dataset (Augmentation column of Table~\ref{tab:dataset_reps}), and on QM9 the topological torsion fingerprint (2,048 features) is the only wide donor. \citet{Bahia2023} reported that combining 2D and 3D descriptors gave many more significant models across six protein--ligand series (461 structures), which they attribute to complementary properties. Their 3D descriptors came from bioactive conformations in the Protein Data Bank, whereas our 3D donors use one generated conformer per molecule.

% TODO: manual review
Donor features are ranked by what the base gets wrong. Inside each outer training fold, an 80/20 internal split keeps each scaffold family or sequence group on one side. A small random forest (50 trees, depth at most 10) is fitted on the base features. Three-fold group-aware cross-validation gives out-of-fold residuals $r_i = y_i - \hat{y}^{\mathrm{oof}}_i$ for the internal-training samples, since in-sample residuals would shrink toward zero. For binary tasks, $\hat{y}$ is the positive-class probability. All donor features are pooled, each is scored by its absolute Pearson correlation with the residuals, $c_j = |\rho_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathbf{x}_j, \mathbf{r})|$, and the top $M = 50$ go forward. Ranking against $\mathbf{y}$ would favor features that repeat what the base captures. Ranking against $\mathbf{r}$ favors features that explain its errors, as in gradient boosting \citep{friedman2001greedy} and least angle regression \citep{efron2004lars}.

% TODO: manual review
Blocks of the top $k \in \{0, 5, 10, 20, 40\}$ ranked features, capped at the number of candidates, are each added to the base. Each is scored with the downstream model on the internal-validation samples. The chosen $k^*$ has the best score, and $k=0$ wins ties, which makes keeping no extras the default. Five block choices instead of about 50 accept-or-reject decisions leave less room to overfit a small validation set \citep{Cawley2010}. The chosen donors and feature positions are stored and re-extracted for the outer fold.

% TODO: manual review
In uncapped mode, extras are appended outside the budget and the final width is $|\mathbf{H}| + k^*$, at most $B + 40$ features. In capped mode they replace the base features with the lowest random forest importance, and thus the width stays at most $B$. Uncapped augmentation and no augmentation differ in width at the same $B$, and a comparison between them mixes better features with more features. Capped mode removes this difference, since each extra replaces a base feature.

% TODO: manual review
Augmentation is not a searched setting: a dataset with donors is searched three times, with no, uncapped and capped augmentation (Table~\ref{tab:aug_conditions}). An earlier search of each whole dataset covered 16 datasets of an earlier list. On none did either augmented condition beat no augmentation by more than 1\% (3\% on datasets under about 1,000 samples). We therefore ran the searches of Chapter~\ref{chap:method} without augmentation on all 20 datasets (Section~\ref{sec:method:decided_grid}). Only the nearest-neighbor search on SMAC colloidal stability and HIC retention keeps uncapped augmentation (Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity}).

% TODO: manual review
The residual ranking has four limitations. $|\rho_{\mathrm{P}}|$ captures only linear relations, one feature at a time. It ignores redundancy: two near-identical donor features can both rank high. Donors are pooled with no per-donor limit, which gives a 2,048-feature donor more chances than a 49-feature one. Finally, with a zero margin over $k=0$, a near-tie on one internal split can admit extras.

% TODO: manual review
\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.78\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{@{}P{2.4cm}P{3.0cm}P{4.6cm}P{3.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
Condition & Final width (features) & What an extra feature must beat & Datasets that run it \\
\midrule
No augmentation & at most $B$ & nothing is offered & all 20 \\
Uncapped & $|\mathbf{H}| + k^*$, at most $B + 40$ & keeping no extras & SMAC colloidal stability and HIC retention, nearest-neighbor search only \\
Capped & at most $B$ & keeping no extras, and the weakest base feature it replaces & none (cut after the earlier search) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[The three augmentation conditions.]{The three augmentation conditions. $B$ is the budget and $|\mathbf{H}|$ the width of the base hybrid, both in features, and $k^*$ is the chosen number of extra features, which are drawn from a small or specialized feature set offered to the hybrid.}
\label{tab:aug_conditions}
\end{table}

\addfile{What each cut removes from the hERG $K_i$ search}
\label{app:add:cut_walkthrough}
% TODO: manual review
Table~\ref{tab:cut_walkthrough} follows one dataset, hERG $K_i$, through the cuts; its earlier search, without augmentation, covered six downstream models including kNN. The best had an RMSE of 0.547 p$K_i$ (SVM, 1,000 features, LightGBM gain, softmax allocation, random forest evaluator, all representations), with a standard deviation of 0.044 p$K_i$ over the five folds. Most of the fitting time removed by keeping only the SVM and the DNN came from XGBoost (35\%) and LightGBM (25\%). For each later cut, the best configuration it removed was behind the best kept by less than the spread over folds. The final grid still contains the best configuration, which is expected, because the cuts were chosen on this same search.

\addfile{Default and tuned model settings on single representations}
\label{app:add:tuning_base_reps}
% TODO: manual review
% RERUN-SWAP-DNN: DNN rows of caco2 (file 2026-10-07T17:07) and herg_ki (2026-10-08T06:05) predate c18ee30 (8 Oct 13:04, DNN predictions capped at the training-label range).
We tested whether tuning helps on one outer fold of QM9, hERG $K_i$, Caco-2 efflux, LogD and Buchwald--Hartwig yield. Each downstream model was given its two best single representations, chosen on that fold's training molecules. The tuner tried the default and 20 draws from grids that reach stronger regularization than the optional search. In this test the DNN was tuned as well, over dropout, learning rate and layer sizes. The tuner scored each setting on 3 inner folds grouped by scaffold family of the training molecules, which kept the held-out fold out of the tuning. Table~\ref{tab:tuning_base_reps} gives the held-out RMSE at both settings. The SVM gained most, on LogD with MHG-GNN. Tuning made the random forest worse on Buchwald--Hartwig yield. At default settings, two repeat fits of the DNN on QM9 with Mol2vec scored 0.487 and 0.495. The DNN rows for Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$ were computed before DNN predictions were capped at the range of the training labels. With one fold, these pairs show direction only.

% [CUTS 2026-10-10, item 17] moved unchanged from Chapter 5, the PCA paragraph of "Against ensembles, and many representations against few".
% The chapter keeps which method wins with each model; the first sentence below is new, so the moved text has its subject.
\addfile{Hybrid against PCA: three experiments that disagree}
\label{app:add:pca_disagreement}
% TODO: manual review
Chapter~\ref{chap:method} compares the hybrid with PCA of all representations joined, at the budget each fold's search picked.
% TODO: manual review
% SOURCE: /Volumes/seagate/kirby_results_2026-10-10b/complexity/c02_complexity_matching_grid/<ds>.json (RERUN-SWAP-DNN; the 20 thesis files are identical to the 10-10 folder)
% TODO: c15 (distances and kernels in the hybrid against its representations and PCA; c15_ml_theory_depth/<ds>.json) is read together with this PCA comparison, as the outline asks (its PCA reading disagreed with c02 and c11). Not written until the DNN re-runs.
The ensemble experiment repeats this comparison on the same folds and agrees on LogD and Caco-2. A third experiment disagrees on both, with the DNN hybrid ahead on every fold, 0.409 against 0.477 RMSE on LogD at 1,257 features. That experiment sets the width as a fixed share of the dataset's molecules, rescales each principal component before the model, and does not cap held-out features at the training range. The two experiments therefore differ in three ways at once, and we cannot say which causes the disagreement. All their DNN results for LogD and Caco-2 predate the DNN correction.

% [CUTS 2026-10-10, item 18] moved unchanged from Chapter 5: three Methods paragraphs of "Transfer, collaboration studies and separate sites"
% (how the site models' predictions are combined; the first and the second/third further experiments) and three results paragraphs with
% Tables tab:method:site_reps, tab:method:flipped_sites and tab:method:cluster_sites from "Features chosen across separate sites".
% The chapter keeps the family-per-site experiment (Table tab:method:family_sites) and its paragraph. Only the heading is new.
\addfile{Three further experiments with separate sites}
\label{app:add:separate_sites}

% TODO: manual review
The site models' predictions are combined in one of two ways. Under distance weighting, each site's vote on a molecule is weighted by how close the molecule is to that site's training molecules. The distance is measured to the 8 nearest molecules in a random 20\% sample of that site's training molecules, on the features the site model uses. Distances are Manhattan by default, whereas the collaborator's code uses Euclidean. Under score weighting, each site has one weight, its own cross-validated score. Only predictions on shared molecules cross a site boundary. The published process then trains a student model on the most confident combined labels.

% TODO: manual review
In the first of three further experiments, labels are made two-class: an efflux ratio of at least 2 on Caco-2, and a LogD of at least 3. Five sites are formed by $k$-means clusters of the molecules. Up to 30\% of each site's training labels are flipped, at different rates on different sites. At each flip level, selection at the sites is compared with the ensembles and the student model of the published process. It is compared as well with one model on the pooled molecules, and with the same selection made on all molecules pooled. A version in which each site first removes the molecules it flags as noisy is not reported, because some sites were then left with too few molecules, groups or classes to score a representation.

% TODO: manual review
In the second experiment, each of five sites holds the same molecules as a different representation. One hybrid over all sites is compared with the best single site and with the published ensembles. In the third, the molecules are split into $k$-means clusters and features are selected per cluster. These features are used either for one model on all molecules or for a separate model per cluster. The same experiment asks whether the clusters agree on which representations matter, against random subsets of the same size. The first and third experiments report MCC on the two-class labels, and the second reports RMSE.

% TODO: manual review
% SOURCE: /Volumes/seagate/kirby_results_2026-10-09/federated/fed02_rep_diversity_silos/{caco2,logd}.json (RERUN-SWAP-DNN)
% NOTE (Chapter 5 check, 2026-10-10): this paragraph had no table; Table tab:method:site_reps added, and the three scores in the last sentence moved into it. The fed03 table's LogD SVM pair printed 0.4382 twice; raw 0.438234 and 0.438223, now five decimals.
When each of five sites holds a different representation, one hybrid over all sites beats the best single site on both datasets with both models (Table~\ref{tab:method:site_reps}). It also beats the collaborator's two ensembles, which combine the sites' predictions, except on Caco-2 with the DNN, where both ensembles have the lower RMSE.

% SOURCE: /Volumes/seagate/kirby_results_2026-10-09/federated/fed01_noise_heterogeneity/caco2.partial.jsonl (RERUN-SWAP-DNN)
% TODO: manual review
% NOTE (table build, 2026-10-10): "It never beats the same selection made with all molecules pooled" was changed: with the SVM at 20% flips selection at each site scores 0.5098 MCC against 0.5073 pooled (same file, mean of the two SVM seeds).
Only Caco-2 has finished this experiment, with three seeds for the DNN and two for the SVM. There, with two-class labels and up to 30\% of each site's labels flipped, selecting features at each site beats the collaborator's ensembles and student model at every flip level (Table~\ref{tab:method:flipped_sites}). With the DNN it stays well ahead of the better ensemble from no flips to 30\% flipped. It beats the same selection made with all molecules pooled only with the SVM at 20\% flips, so keeping the data apart still costs something. With the DNN at 30\% flips the two are almost equal.

% TODO: manual review
We selected features separately in each of five $k$-means clusters of Caco-2 and fitted one model on all molecules. This matches one global selection, while fitting a separate model per cluster is worse (Table~\ref{tab:method:cluster_sites}). The clusters agree less on which representations matter than random parts of the same size do, measured by mean rank correlation.

\subsection{Small Molecules}\label{app:datasets:smallmol}
% [notes moved to thesis_notes_moved.md]

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
Three steps are shared by several small-molecule datasets. The first is structure standardization with RDKit \citep{rdkit}: a general cleanup, keeping the largest fragment, neutralizing charges and selecting the canonical tautomer, in that order. Molecules that then contain a metal atom are rejected. Standardization is applied to hERG $K_i$, LogD and Caco-2; the Biogen sets, QM9 and the hERG blocker sets are only checked to parse in RDKit.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
The second removes duplicate molecules by InChIKey \citep{Heller2015}, keeping the first occurrence, so that one molecule cannot sit in both a training and a test fold. It is applied to hERG $K_i$, LogD, Caco-2, the two Biogen sets and QM9, and to the hERG blocker sets only across sites (below). The third is fold grouping by Bemis--Murcko scaffold \citep{Bemis1996} (Chapter~\ref{chap:method}).

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [RESTORE 2026-09-30] AF-1 AF-2
\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{hERG $K_i$.}{hERG Ki.}} hERG records (ChEMBL target CHEMBL240) were retrieved from ChEMBL \citep{Zdrazil2023}, keeping binding assays (assay type B) with standard type $K_i$, a pChEMBL value, an exact relation (``$=$'') and no curator data-validity comment. The label is the pChEMBL value, $\mathrm{p}K_i = -\log_{10} K_i$ with $K_i$ in molar units. Repeat measurements of one compound are merged into their median, and compounds whose repeats have a standard deviation above 1.0 p$K_i$ unit are removed, following \citet{landrum2024}. This gives 1,415 compounds, 226 of them measured more than once and none removed by the standard-deviation rule. After standardization, InChIKey deduplication removes 3, leaving 1,412 compounds with p$K_i$ from 4.07 to 9.41. Because repeats are merged on ChEMBL's own SMILES before standardization, two ChEMBL entries that standardize to the same molecule are not merged by median; the InChIKey step keeps the first of them. The file read by the experiments was pulled on 1 February 2026; the ChEMBL release was not recorded at the time. Re-running the same query in September 2026 against ChEMBL release 37 gives a byte-identical file of 1,415 compounds.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{LogD and Caco-2 efflux.} Both come from the training split of the OpenADMET--ExpansionRx challenge data on Hugging Face \citep{openadmet}. For each endpoint, rows without a value are dropped, structures are standardized, and rows that standardize to the same SMILES are merged into their median label. The unitless efflux ratio is transformed to $\log_{10}$. Labels are clipped at the 1st and 99th percentiles of each dataset, $-1.0$ and $4.5$ for LogD and $-0.319$ and $1.679$ for the $\log_{10}$ efflux ratio. This sets 116 LogD and 45 Caco-2 labels to a limit before any noise is added in Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity}. InChIKey deduplication removes nothing.
% TODO(lit): pH at which the OpenADMET LogD values were measured (not stated on the Hugging Face page).

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{Rat liver microsome clearance and MDR1--MDCK efflux.} These are two endpoints of the Biogen ADME set of 3,521 compounds \citep{fang2023}, from Polaris as \texttt{biogen/adme-fang-v1} \citep{polaris}: intrinsic clearance $\mathrm{CL_{int}}$ in mL/min/kg and an efflux ratio, both released and used as $\log_{10}$ values. Rows without a value, or whose SMILES RDKit cannot parse, are dropped, and InChIKey deduplication removes one molecule from each. There is no standardization, median merge or clipping.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [RESTORE 2026-09-30] AF-3
\paragraph{QM9.} QM9 \citep{Ramakrishnan2014, qm9_dataset} was read through PyTorch Geometric \citep{pytorchGeometric}, which distributes 130,831 molecules. Of these, 129,428 parse in RDKit. From these, 10,000 molecules were drawn once at random with seed 42, so the subsample is a random draw of molecules, not a random split. InChIKey deduplication removes 22, leaving 9,978. The label is the HOMO--LUMO gap in eV, from 1.88 to 11.37 eV in this sample.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [TRIM4 2026-09-30] cut the count of disagreeing blocker calls
\paragraph{hERG blockers held at separate sites.} The separate-site experiments of Chapter~\ref{chap:method} use two further hERG sets from Lhasa Limited \citep{Bassani2023, Hanser2023}: a training set of 7,772 molecules (3,630 blockers, 46.7\%) and a test set of 4,383 molecules (1,084 blockers). Each label is Lhasa's own binary blocker call, made under different rules in the two sets. Both are proprietary (Section~\ref{app:availability}). The hERG $K_i$ set acts as a third site, with a compound counted as a blocker when $\mathrm{p}K_i \ge 6.0$, giving 467 blockers and 945 non-blockers. Molecules are matched by InChIKey across the sites, in the order hERG $K_i$, training set, test set, keeping the first copy. This removes 166 training and 170 test molecules, all of them also in the hERG $K_i$ set; the two Lhasa sets share none.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [RESTORE 2026-09-30] AF-4
\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{hERG IC$_{50}$.}{hERG IC50.}} This set is used only in Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity}, where its repeat measurements and curator flags serve as real evidence of label error. It keeps every measurement as its own row. The query takes ChEMBL records for CHEMBL240 with standard type IC$_{50}$ in nM and an exact relation \citep{Zdrazil2023}, dropping records with a missing or non-positive value. The label is $\mathrm{pIC_{50}} = 9 - \log_{10}(\mathrm{IC_{50}}/\mathrm{nM})$. Assay type is not filtered. Of the 12,010 records, 10,219 come from binding assays, 1,543 from toxicity assays, 217 from functional assays and 31 from ADME assays, as ChEMBL classifies them. The 301 records with a curator data-validity comment, on 215 molecules, are kept and marked. With no standardization, median merge or InChIKey step, the result is 12,008 measurements of 9,802 molecules. Folds are grouped by scaffold, so no molecule is split between training and test.

\subsection{Chemical Reactions}\label{app:datasets:reactions}

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
Both reaction datasets come from the high-throughput Buchwald--Hartwig C--N coupling experiments of \citet{Ahneman2018}, which varied aryl halide, palladium ligand, base and additive. The label is the yield in \%, from 0 to 100. One sample is a whole reaction, written as its reactants and product.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{Buchwald--Hartwig yield.} This is the version released with \citet{Schwaller2021a}, from that paper's public repository: 3,955 reactions. Each is written as aryl halide, 4-methylaniline, ligand, base and additive going to a product, generated from the aryl halide by a C--N coupling template that succeeds on all 3,955. They fall into 22 additive groups of 178 to 180 reactions.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{Buchwald--Hartwig plates.} This is the data table of the public repository of \citet{Ahneman2018}: 4,599 rows over three plates, with product SMILES from the file. The 287 rows without an aryl halide are dropped. The 180 remaining rows without an additive form their own group, giving 24 additive groups of 177 to 180 reactions. The 4,312 reactions kept include all 3,955 of the first set, with the same aryl halide, ligand, base, additive and yield, plus 357 more: the 180 without an additive and 177 with 5-phenyl-1,2,4-oxadiazole as the additive.

\subsection{Antibodies}\label{app:datasets:antibodies}
% [notes moved to thesis_notes_moved.md]

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [RESTORE 2026-09-30] AF-5
The Koenig and nanobody sets were downloaded as CSV files from the FLAb benchmark repository \citep{Chungyoun2024}, the humanness set from the BioPhi publication repository and the trastuzumab set from its own repository. The FLAb and BioPhi files come from each repository's main branch, with no commit pinned. Each file has been changed only once. The Koenig and nanobody files were changed in FLAb commits \texttt{c41e56e} and \texttt{005e6c1}, both on 6 August 2025, and the two humanness files in BioPhi commit \texttt{c0797f2} on 24 August 2021. The local copies of the Koenig and humanness files are identical to those commits.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
The nanobody and humanness sets are grouped by sequence identity, defined for sequences $a$ and $b$ as
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{id}(a,b) = 1 - \frac{\mathrm{lev}(a,b)}{\max(|a|,|b|)},
\end{equation}
where $\mathrm{lev}$ is the Levenshtein (edit) distance and $|a|$ is the length in residues. Following \citet{Kenlay2024}, sequences are clustered by single linkage at $\mathrm{id} \ge 0.95$. The 672 nanobodies fall into 309 clusters and the 564 humanness antibodies into 461.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{Koenig expression.} This deep mutational scan of the anti-VEGF antibody G6 \citep{Koenig2017}, taken from FLAb, has 4,275 variants with both chains (heavy chains of 120 residues, light chains of 108). Each carries exactly one mutation, 2,261 in the heavy chain and 2,014 in the light chain, at 225 distinct positions. The label is the unitless expression enrichment ratio, from 0.00098 to 3.178.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [TRIM4 2026-09-30] cut the measurement-method sentence
\paragraph{Nanobody thermostability.} This is the NbThermo set \citep{ValdesTresanco2023}, taken from FLAb: melting temperatures $T_m$ from 39.0 to 98.2 $^\circ$C for 672 single-domain antibodies with no light chain. The 672 rows hold 556 distinct sequences; 116 appear twice, and 106 of those pairs carry different melting temperatures, up to 8.8 $^\circ$C apart. These repeats are kept, so the labels carry disagreement between measurements before any noise is added.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [TRIM4 2026-09-30] cut the species breakdown of the non-human class
% [RESTORE 2026-09-30] AF-6
\paragraph{Humanness.} This is the list of 564 therapeutic antibodies from IMGT/mAb-DB released with BioPhi \citep{Prihoda2022}, using the first sequence listed for each. An antibody is labeled human (1) only when its species field is exactly ``Human'' (198 antibodies). The other 366 are labeled non-human (0). The 564 antibodies hold 506 distinct sequences, mostly because an antibody and its drug conjugate share a sequence. One sequence is shared by three antibodies with different labels, two humanized and one human. All three sit in one identity cluster, so they never fall on both sides of a split.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [RESTORE 2026-09-30] AF-7
\paragraph{Trastuzumab--HER2 binding.} This is the library of trastuzumab variants in the ten-residue CDR-H3 (the third complementarity-determining region of the heavy chain), screened for HER2 binding by \citet{Chinery2024}. The released training, validation and test files are joined and re-split. All 524,346 variants have a 10-residue CDR-H3 and are kept; 32.8\% are binders. From these, 5,000 variants were drawn once with seed 42, stratified so that each combination of label and number of mutated CDR-H3 residues keeps its share; the sample holds 1,639 binders (32.8\%). Each 120-residue heavy chain is rebuilt by inserting the variant CDR-H3 into the trastuzumab framework, and paired with the fixed trastuzumab light chain.

\subsection{Nucleotide Sequences}\label{app:datasets:nucleotides}

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [RESTORE 2026-09-30] AF-8
Both nucleotide tasks come from the revised downstream tasks of the Nucleotide Transformer \citep{DallaTorre2025} on Hugging Face (\nolinkurl{InstaDeepAI/nucleotide_transformer_downstream_tasks_revised}). The download is not pinned to a revision; the files used are those of revision \texttt{851f994}, dated 30 June 2025, the latest at the time of writing. The released training and test files are merged, and folds are grouped by the chromosome read from each sequence's name. Nothing else is filtered. Both tasks cover chromosomes 1 to 21, X and Y; chromosome 22 is absent.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [RESTORE 2026-09-30] AF-9
\paragraph{H3K4me3.} Each sample is a 1,000-base sequence, labeled by whether it carries the H3K4me3 histone mark. The merged files hold 18,244 sequences, 9,122 per class. The dataset card lists 30,000 training sequences; the count here is that of the files.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
\paragraph{TATA promoter.} Each sample is a 300-base sequence, labeled by whether it is a promoter with a TATA box. The merged files hold 5,274 sequences, 2,637 per class.

\section{Data and code availability}\label{app:availability}
% [notes moved to thesis_notes_moved.md]
% TODO: needs manual review (whole chapter written by Claude)

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% [TRIM 2026-09-30] shortened
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [TRIM4 2026-09-30] cut the QM9-pipeline mention (the qsar_qm_models paragraph keeps it)
The software behind this thesis includes NoiseInject, the label-noise package released with Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}, and the KIRBy repository, which implements the adaptive hybrid representation and holds the experiments of Chapters~\ref{chap:flaws}, \ref{chap:method} and~\ref{chap:similarity} and the assay-dataset runs of Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}.

% [TRIM4 2026-09-30] shortened to the license and a pointer to Chapter 2
% TODO: review, needs manual review
NoiseInject is available as an open-source Python package under an MIT license \citep{noiseinject}. Its noise conditions and metrics are described in Section~\ref{sec:noise_injection}.

\begin{itemize}
    \item Project name: NoiseInject
    \item Project home page: \url{https://github.com/adpunt/noise_inject}
    \item Archived version: \url{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.20532710}
    \item Version: 1.0.0
    \item Operating system(s): Platform independent
    \item Programming language: Python 3.7 or later
    \item Other requirements: NumPy, pandas, SciPy and scikit-learn; see the repository's \texttt{requirements.txt}
    \item License: MIT
    \item Any restrictions to use by non-academics: None
\end{itemize}

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% [TRIM 2026-09-30] shortened
% TODO: review, needs manual review
The QM9 results of Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}, with that chapter's hyperparameter tuning, variance decomposition and figure scripts, come from a separate repository in Python and Rust, described in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} under ``QM9 Data Pipeline''.

\begin{itemize}
    \item Project name: qsar\_qm\_models
    \item Project home page: \url{https://github.com/adpunt/qsar_qm_models}
    \item Archived version: not yet archived
    \item Operating system(s): Linux and macOS
    \item Programming language: Python and Rust (2021 edition)
    \item Other requirements: RDKit installed through conda, to which the Rust program links; PyTorch and PyTorch Geometric
    \item License: GNU GPL v3.0
    \item Any restrictions to use by non-academics: those of the GNU GPL v3.0
\end{itemize}

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
KIRBy is written in Python and works on feature matrices computed beforehand, so the hybrid search applies to representations from any domain. It holds the hybrid search, loaders for the datasets in Section~\ref{app:datasets}, generators for the representations, the similarity analyses and learning-with-noisy-labels methods of Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity}, and every experiment script. Label noise is added through NoiseInject, so the noise definitions match Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}.

\begin{itemize}
    \item Project name: KIRBy
    \item Project home page: \url{https://github.com/adpunt/KIRBy}
    \item Archived version: not yet archived
    \item Operating system(s): Linux and macOS
    \item Programming language: Python 3.8 or later; several pretrained encoders need Python 3.10 or later
    \item Other requirements: NumPy, pandas, SciPy, scikit-learn, and RDKit installed through conda; each application area has an optional install
    \item License: to be set on release
    \item Any restrictions to use by non-academics: to be set on release
\end{itemize}


% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
All datasets but one are public. QM9 \citep{Ramakrishnan2014} is deposited on Figshare \citep{qm9_dataset}. The LogD and Caco-2 efflux data are from the OpenADMET--ExpansionRx release under CC-BY-4.0 \citep{openadmet}. The hERG $K_i$ data come from ChEMBL \citep{Zdrazil2023}, released under CC BY-SA 3.0, and can be regenerated with the protocol in Section~\ref{app:datasets}. The rat liver microsome clearance and MDR1--MDCK efflux data are the Biogen ADME set \citep{fang2023}, on Polaris \citep{polaris} under CC-BY-4.0. The reaction-yield, antibody and nucleotide data come from the public releases cited in Section~\ref{app:datasets}. Pretrained model weights come from their original sources and are not redistributed.

% [TRIM3 2026-09-30] simplified
% TODO: review, needs manual review
% [TRIM4 2026-09-30] cut the molecule counts, pointer to Appendix A
The exception is Lhasa Limited's curated hERG training and test sets \citep{Hanser2025} (Section~\ref{app:datasets}), used for feature selection with data held at separate sites (Chapter~\ref{chap:method}). They are proprietary and not redistributed. The comparison with FLuID also cannot be run without Lhasa's FLuID code. Every other result in this thesis can be reproduced from public data and the code above.
